Encoding Tight Typing in a Unified Framework
Delia Kesner, Andrés Viso

Abstract
This paper explores how the intersection type theories of call-by-name (CBN) and call-by-value (CBV) can be unified in a more general framework provided by call-by-push-value (CBPV). Indeed, we propose tight type systems for CBN and CBV that can be both encoded in a unique tight type system for CBPV. All such systems are quantitative, i.e. they provide exact information about the length of normalization sequences to normal form as well as the size of these normal forms. Moreover, the length of reduction sequences are discriminated according to their multiplicative and exponential nature, a concept inherited from linear logic. Last but not least, it is possible to extract quantitative measures for CBN and CBV from their corresponding encodings in CBPV.

discussions. We also thank B. Accattoli, A. Bucciarelli, and A. Ríos for
constructive remarks. This work has been partially supported by IRP SINFIN.

1
Introduction

Every programming language implements a particular evaluation strategy which
specifies when and how parameters are evaluated during function calls. For
example, in
call-by-value (CBV)
, the argument is evaluated before being
passed to the function, while in
call-by-name (CBN)
the argument is
substituted directly into the function body, so that the argument may never be
evaluated, or may be re-evaluated several times. CBN and CBV have always been
studied independently, by developing different techniques for one and the
other, until the remarkable observation that they are two different instances
of a more general framework introduced by Girard’s
Linear Logic (LL)
.
Their (logical) duality (“CBN is
dual
to CBV”) was understood
later
[
18
,
17
]
. And their rewriting semantics were finally

unified
by the
call-by-push-value (CBPV)
paradigm —introduced
by P.B. Levy
[
40
,
41
]
—, a formalism being able to capture
different functional languages/evaluation strategies.

A typical aspect that one wants to compare between two different evaluation
strategies is the
number of steps
that are necessary to get a result.
Such numbers are extracted from different models of computation
and should be then measured by
compatible
instruments, either by
means of common quantitative tools, or by a precise transformation between
them
1
1

1

Think for example about cm and inches.
. Thus, we provide a

uniform
tool to measure quantitative information extracted from the
evaluation of programs in different programming languages (namely CBN and
CBV).

More concretely, we introduce typing systems capturing
qualitative
and

quantitative
information of programs. From a qualitative point of view,
the typing systems characterize termination of programs,
i.e.
a program
p
is
typable if and only if
p
terminates. From a quantitative point of view, the
typing systems provide
exact/tight
information about the
number of
steps
needed to get a result, as well as the
size
of this result. All
these questions are addressed in the general framework of CBPV, being able to
encode CBN and CBV, both from a static and a dynamic point of view.
Dynamically, CBN and CBV evaluation strategies are known to be encodable by the
rewriting semantics of CBPV. Statically, we define tight typing systems
providing quantitative information for CBN and CBV, that can be seen as
particular cases of the tight quantitative typing system behind the unified
framework of CBPV. We now explain all these concepts in more detail.

Quantitative Types.
Quantitative typing systems are often specified by
non-idempotent intersection types inspired by the relational semantics of
LL
[
30
,
11
]
, as pioneered by
[
28
]
. This
connection makes non-idempotent types not only a qualitative typing tool to
reason about programming languages, but mainly a quantitative one, being able
to specify properties related to the consumption of resources, a remarkable
investigation pioneered by the seminal de Carvalho’s PhD
thesis
[
19
]
(see also
[
21
]
). Thus,
qualitatively, a non-idempotent typing system is able to fully
characterise
normalisation, in the sense that a term
t
is typable if and
only if
t
is normalising. More interestingly, quantitative typing systems
also provide
upper bounds
, in the sense that the length of any
reduction sequence from
t
to normal form
plus
the size of this normal
form is
bounded
by the size of the type derivation of
t
. Therefore,
typability characterises normalisation in a qualitative as well as in a
quantitative way, but only provides upper bounds. Several papers explore
bounded measures of non-idempotent types for different higher order languages.
Some references
are
[
24
,
31
,
4
,
3
,
35
,
14
,
20
,
33
,
23
,
22
,
13
,
43
]
.

In this satisfactory enough? A major observation concerning
\beta
-reduction
in
\lambda
-calculus is that the size of normal forms can be

exponentially
bigger than the number of steps needed to reach these
normal forms. This means that bounding the sum of these two integers
at
the same
time is too rough, and not very relevant from a quantitative point of
view. Fortunately, it is possible to extract better (
i.e.
independent and exact)
measures from a non-idempotent intersection type system. A crucial point to
obtain
exact measures
, instead of upper bounds, is to consider

minimal
type
derivations
[
19
,
9
,
23
]
. Therefore,

upper bounds
for time
plus
size can be refined into

independent exact measures
for time
and

size
[
1
]
. More precisely, the quantitative typing systems are
now equipped with constants and
counters
, together with an appropriate
notion of
tightness
, which encodes minimality of type derivations. For
any
tight
type derivation
\Phi
of a term
t
ending with (independent)
counters
(\mathit{b},\mathit{s})
, it is now possible to show that
t
is normalisable
in
\mathit{b}
steps and its normal form has size
\mathit{s}
, so that the type
system is able to
guess
the number of steps to normal form as well as
the size of this normal form. The opposite direction also holds: if
t

normalises in
\mathit{b}
steps to a normal form size
\mathit{s}
, then it is
possible to tightly type
t
by using (independent) counters
(\mathit{b},\mathit{s})
.

In this paper we design tight quantitative type systems that are also capable
to discriminate between
multiplicative
and
exponential
evaluation
steps to normal form, two conceptual notions coming from LL:

multiplicative
steps are essentially those that (linearly) reconfigure
proofs/terms/programs, while
exponential
steps are the only ones that
are potentially able to erase/duplicate other objects. As a consequence, for
any
tight
type derivation
\Phi
of
t
ending with (independent)
counters
(\mathit{m},\mathit{e},\mathit{s})
, the term
t
is normalisable in
\mathit{m}

multiplicative and
\mathit{e}
exponential steps to a normal form having size

\mathit{s}
. The opposite direction also holds.

Call-by-push-value.
CBPV extends the
\lambda
-calculus with two
primitives
\mathtt{thunk}
and
\mathtt{force}
distinguishing between

values
and
computations
: the former freezes the execution of a
term (
i.e.
turns a computation into a value) while the latter fires again a
frozen term (
i.e.
turns a value into a computation). These primitives allow to
capture the duality between CBN and CBV by conveniently labelling a

\lambda
-term with
\mathtt{thunk}/\mathtt{force}
to pause/resume the
evaluation of a subterm. Thus, CBPV provides a
unique
and
general

formalism capturing different functional strategies, and allowing to

uniformly
study operational and denotational semantics of different
programming languages through a single tool.

In this paper we model the CBPV paradigm by using the

\lambda!
-calculus
[
12
]
, based on the
bang calculus

introduced in
[
26
]
, which in turn extends ideas by
T. Ehrhard
[
25
]
. The granularity of the
\lambda!
-calculus,
expressed with both
explicit substitutions
and
reduction at a
distance
(details in Sec.
5
), clearly allows to differentiate
between
multiplicative
and
exponential
steps, as in LL. The
corresponding CBN and CBV strategies also follow this pattern: it is possible
to distinguish the multiplicative steps that only reconfigure pieces of syntax,
from the exponential steps used to implement erasure and duplication of terms.

Contributions.
We first define deterministic strategies for CBN and
CBV that are able to discriminate between multiplicative and exponential steps
(Sec.
2
).

We then formulate tight typing systems for both CBN (Sec.
3
)
and CBV (Sec.
4
), called respectively
\mathcal{N}
and

\mathcal{V}
. System
\mathcal{N}
is a direct extension of Gardner’s
system
[
28
]
, while system
\mathcal{V}
is completely new, and
constitutes one of the major contributions of this paper. A key feature of
system
\mathcal{V}
is its ability to distinguish between the two different
roles that variables may play in CBV depending on the context where they are
placed,
i.e.
to be a placeholder for a
value
or the head of a

neutral
term. We show that both systems implement independent measures
for time and size, and that they are quantitatively sound and complete. More
precisely, we show that tight (
i.e.
minimal) typing derivations in such systems
(exactly) quantitatively characterise normalisation,
i.e.
if
\Phi
is a

tight
type derivation of
t
in system
\mathcal{N}
(resp.

\mathcal{V}
), ending with counters
(\mathit{m},\mathit{e},\mathit{s})
, then there
exists a CBN (resp. CBV) normal form
p
of size
\mathit{s}
such that
t
reduces
to
p
by using exactly
\mathit{m}
multiplicative steps and
\mathit{e}
exponential
steps. The converse, giving quantitative completeness of the approach, also
holds.

Sec.
5
recalls the bang calculus at a distance
\lambda!
and
Sec.
6
presents its associated tight type system
\mathcal{B}
,
together with their respective quantitative sound and complete properties. The
untyped CBN/CBV translations into
\lambda!
are recalled in
Sec.
7
, while the typed translations are defined and
discussed in Sec.
8
, the other major contribution of
this work. Through these typed encodings,
the counters of the
source and target derivations are related.
This makes it possible to give the precise cost of our
typed translations, as well as to extract quantitative
measures for CBN and CBV from their corresponding encodings in CBPV.

Related Work.
For CBN, non-idempotent types were introduced
by
[
28
]
, their quantitative power was extensively studied
in
[
19
,
20
]
, and their tight extensions
in
[
9
,
1
]
. For CBV, non-idempotent types were
introduced in
[
24
]
, and extensively studied,

e.g.
[
31
,
3
]
. A tight extension being able to count
reduction steps was recently defined in
[
4
,
39
]
for a
special version of (closed) CBV, but it is not clear how this could be encoded
in a linear logic based CBPV framework. Another non-idempotent type system was
also recently introduced for CBV
[
42
,
34
]
, it is not
tight and does not translate to CBPV.

A (non-tight) quantitative type system for the bang calculus, based on a
relational model, can be found in
[
32
]
. Another relational model
that can be seen as a non-tight system for CBPV was introduced
by
[
16
]
. Following ideas
in
[
19
,
9
,
1
]
, a type system

\mathcal{E}
was proposed in
[
12
]
to fully exploit tight
quantitative aspects of the
\lambda!
-calculus:
independent exact
measures
for time and size are guessed by the types system. However, no
relation between tightness for CBN/CBV and tightness for the

\lambda!
-calculus are studied in
op.cite
. This papers fills this gap.

The discrimination between multiplicative and exponential steps by means of
tight quantitative types can be found
e.g.
in CBN
[
1
]
,
call-by-need
[
4
]
, and languages with pattern-matching
primitives
[
7
]
.

2
Call-by-Name and Call-by-Value

This section introduces the CBN and CBV specifications being able to
distinguish between multiplicative and exponential steps, as in linear logic.
Given a countably infinite set
\mathcal{X}
of variables
x,y,z,\ldots
,
we consider the following grammars for terms (
\mathcal{T_{\lambda}}
), values and
contexts:

(Terms)

t,u,r

::=

v\mid{t}\,{u}\mid{t}[{x}\backslash{u}]

(Values)

v

::=

x\in\mathcal{X}\mid\lambda{x}.{t}

(Contexts)

\mathtt{C}

::=

\mathtt{L}\mid\mathtt{N}\mid\mathtt{V}

(List Contexts)

\mathtt{L}

::=

\Box\mid{\mathtt{L}}[{x}\backslash{t}]

(CBN Contexts)

\mathtt{N}

::=

\Box\mid{\mathtt{N}}\,{t}\mid\lambda{x}.{\mathtt{N}}\mid{\mathtt{N}}[{x}\backslash{u}]

(CBV Contexts)

\mathtt{V}

::=

\Box\mid{\mathtt{V}}\,{t}\mid{t}\,{\mathtt{V}}\mid{\mathtt{V}}[{x}\backslash{u}]\mid{t}[{x}\backslash{\mathtt{V}}]

A terms of the form
{t}[{x}\backslash{u}]
is a
closure
, and

[{x}\backslash{u}]
an
explicit substitution
(ES). Special terms are

{\tt I}=\lambda{z}.{z}
,
{\tt K}=\lambda{x}.{\lambda{y}.{x}}
,
\Delta=\lambda{x}.{{x}\,{x}}
, and
\Omega={\Delta}\,{\Delta}
. We use

{\mathtt{C}}\langle{t}\rangle
for the term obtained by replacing the hole
\Box
of

\mathtt{C}
by
t
.
Free
and
bound
variables, as well as

\alpha
-conversion, are defined as expected. In particular,

\mathtt{fv}({{t}[{x}\backslash{u}]})\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textit{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}$}}\mathtt{fv}({t})\setminus\{{x}\}\cup\mathtt{fv}({u})
,

\mathtt{fv}({\lambda{x}.{t}})\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textit{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}$}}\mathtt{fv}({t})\setminus\{{x}\}
,

\mathtt{bv}({{t}[{x}\backslash{u}]})\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textit{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}$}}\mathtt{bv}({t})\cup\{{x}\}\cup\mathtt{bv}({u})
and

\mathtt{bv}({\lambda{x}.{t}})\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textit{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}$}}\mathtt{bv}({t})\cup\{{x}\}
. The notation

{{t}\left\{{x}\backslash{u}\right\}}
is used for the (capture-free)
meta-level

substitution operation, defined, as usual, modulo
\alpha
-conversion. Special
predicates are used to distinguish different kinds of terms surrounded by ES:

\mathsf{abs}(t)
iff
t={\mathtt{L}}\langle{\lambda{x}.{u}}\rangle
,
\mathsf{app}(t)
iff
t={\mathtt{L}}\langle{{r}\,{u}}\rangle
and
\mathsf{var}(t)
iff
t={\mathtt{L}}\langle{x}\rangle
. Finally,
\mathsf{val}(t)
iff
\mathsf{abs}(t)
or
\mathsf{var}(t)
.

As mentioned in the introduction, our aim is to count the reduction steps by
distinguishing their multiplicative and exponential nature. To achieve this,
the standard specifications of CBN/CBV are not adequate, so we need to
consider alternative appropriate definitions
[
6
]
making use of the following three
different rewriting rules:

\begin{array}[]{rlll}\mbox{({\normalsize{d}}istant {\normalsize{B}}eta)}&{{\mathtt{L}}\langle{\lambda{x}.{t}}\rangle}\,{u}&\mathrel{\mapsto_{\mathtt{dB}}}&{\mathtt{L}}\langle{{t}[{x}\backslash{u}]}\rangle\\
\mbox{({\normalsize{s}}ubstitute term)}&{t}[{x}\backslash{u}]&\mathrel{\mapsto_{\mathtt{sn}}}&{{t}\left\{{x}\backslash{u}\right\}}\\
\mbox{({\normalsize{s}}ubstitute {v}alue)}&{t}[{x}\backslash{{\mathtt{L}}\langle{v}\rangle}]&\mathrel{\mapsto_{\mathtt{sv}}}&{\mathtt{L}}\langle{{{t}\left\{{x}\backslash{v}\right\}}}\rangle\end{array}

Rule
\mathtt{dB}
fires

\beta
-reduction
at a distance
by combining the two more elementary
rules:
{(\lambda{x}.{t})}\,{u}\mathrel{\mapsto}{t}[{x}\backslash{u}]
and

{{\mathtt{L}}\langle{t}\rangle}\,{u}\mathrel{\mapsto}{\mathtt{L}}\langle{{t}\,{u}}\rangle
, where the second one is a
structural/permutation rule pushing out ES that may block
\beta
-redexes. Rule

\mathtt{sn}
implements standard substitution, while
\mathtt{sv}
restricts substitution
to values and acts
at a distance
by combining the two more elementary
rules:
{t}[{x}\backslash{v}]\mathrel{\mapsto}{{t}\left\{{x}\backslash{v}\right\}}
and

{t}[{x}\backslash{{\mathtt{L}}\langle{v}\rangle}]\mathrel{\mapsto}{\mathtt{L}}\langle{{t}[{x}\backslash{v}]}\rangle
. The
call-by-name
reduction
relation
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}}
is the closure by contexts
\mathtt{N}
of the
rules
\mathtt{dB}
and
\mathtt{sn}
, while the
call-by-value
reduction
relation
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{v}}
is the closure by contexts
\mathtt{V}
of the
rules
\mathtt{dB}
and
\mathtt{sv}
. Equivalently,

\mathbin{\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}}}:=\mathtt{N}(\mathrel{\mapsto_{\mathtt{dB}}}\cup\mathrel{\mapsto_{\mathtt{sn}}})\quad
and
\quad\mathbin{\rightarrow_{\mathtt{v}}}:=\mathtt{V}(\mathrel{\mapsto_{\mathtt{dB}}}\cup\mathrel{\mapsto_{\mathtt{sv}}})

The resulting CBN/CBV formulations are now based on distinguished

multiplicative
(
cf.
\mathtt{dB}
) and
exponential
(
cf.
\mathtt{sn}
and
\mathtt{sv}
) steps, called resp.
\mathtt{m}
-steps and
\mathtt{e}
-steps,
thus inheriting the nature of cut elimination rules in LL. Notice that the
number of
\mathtt{m}
and
\mathtt{e}
-steps in a normalization sequence is not always
the same:
e.g.
{x}[{x}\backslash{y}]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}}y
has only
one
\mathtt{e}
-step, and
{(\lambda{x}.{x})}\,{({z}\,{{\tt I}})}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{v}}{x}[{x}\backslash{{z}\,{{\tt I}}}]
has only one

\mathtt{m}
-step. We write
t\not\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}}
(resp.
t\not\rightarrow_{\mathtt{v}}
), and call
t
an
\mathtt{n}
-normal form

(resp.
\mathtt{v}
-normal form
), if
t
cannot be reduced by means
of
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}}
(resp.
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{v}}
). Both CBN and CBV
are non-deterministic:
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}}u
and
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}}s
does not necessarily implies
u=s
. But both calculi enjoy confluence,
notably because their rules are orthogonal
[
38
,
37
]
. Moreover, in
each calculus, it is easy to show that any two different reduction paths to
normal form have the same number of multiplicative and exponential steps.

CBN is to be understood as
head

reduction
[
8
]
,
i.e.
reduction does not take place in
arguments of applications, while CBV corresponds to
open
CBV
reduction
[
6
,
2
]
,
i.e.
reduction does not
take place inside abstractions. The sets of

\mathtt{n}/\mathtt{v}
-normal forms can be alternatively
characterised by the following grammars
[
12
]
:

(CBN Neutral)

\mathsf{ne}_{\mathtt{n}}

::=

x\in\mathcal{X}\mid{\mathsf{ne}_{\mathtt{n}}}\,{t}

(CBN Normal)

\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{n}}

::=

\lambda{x}.{\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{n}}}\mid\mathsf{ne}_{\mathtt{n}}

(CBV Variable)

\mathsf{vr}_{\mathtt{v}}

::=

x\in\mathcal{X}\mid{\mathsf{vr}_{\mathtt{v}}}[{x}\backslash{\mathsf{ne}_{\mathtt{v}}}]

(CBV Neutral)

\mathsf{ne}_{\mathtt{v}}

::=

{\mathsf{vr}_{\mathtt{v}}}\,{\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{v}}}\mid{\mathsf{ne}_{\mathtt{v}}}\,{\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{v}}}\mid{\mathsf{ne}_{\mathtt{v}}}[{x}\backslash{\mathsf{ne}_{\mathtt{v}}}]

(CBV Normal)

\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{v}}

::=

\lambda{x}.{t}\mid\mathsf{vr}_{\mathtt{v}}\mid\mathsf{ne}_{\mathtt{v}}\mid{\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{v}}}[{x}\backslash{\mathsf{ne}_{\mathtt{v}}}]

In contrast to CBN, variables are left out of the definition of neutral terms
for the CBV case, since they are now considered as values. However, even if
CBV variables are not neutral terms, neutral terms are necessarily headed by
a variable, so that variables play a double role which is difficult to be
distinguished by means of an intersection type system. We will come back to
this point in Sec.
4
. Excluding variables from the set of
values brings a remarkable speed up in implementations of
CBV
[
39
]
, but goes beyond the logical Girard’s
translation of CBV into LL, which is the main topic of this paper.
Our chosen approach allows both CBN and CBV neutral terms to translate to
neutral terms of the
\lambda!
-calculus (
cf.
Sec.
5
).

Deterministic Strategies for CBN and CBV.

As a technical tool, in order to count the reduction steps of CBN/CBV we first
fix a deterministic version for them. The reduction relation
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}
is a
deterministic version of
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}}
defined as:

\displaystyle{{{\mathtt{L}}\langle{\lambda{x}.{t}}\rangle}\,{u}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}{\mathtt{L}}\langle{{t}[{x}\backslash{u}]}\rangle}

\displaystyle{{t}[{x}\backslash{u}]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}{{t}\left\{{x}\backslash{u}\right\}}}

\displaystyle\penalty t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}s\mbox{ and }\neg\mathsf{abs}(t)

\displaystyle{{t}\,{u}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}{s}\,{u}}

\displaystyle\penalty t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}s

\displaystyle{\lambda{x}.{t}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}\lambda{x}.{u}}

Similarly, the reduction relation
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dv}}
is a deterministic version of

\rightarrow_{\mathtt{v}}
defined as:

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 32.32312pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vphantom{\mathsf{abs}(t)}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=89.84073pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{{\mathtt{L}}\langle{\lambda{x}.{t}}\rangle}\,{u}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dv}}{\mathtt{L}}\langle{{t}[{x}\backslash{u}]}\rangle$}}}}\qquad{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 35.55453pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vphantom{\mathsf{abs}(t)}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=96.30356pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{t}[{x}\backslash{{\mathtt{L}}\langle{v}\rangle}]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dv}}{\mathtt{L}}\langle{{{t}\left\{{x}\backslash{v}\right\}}}\rangle$}}}}\quad{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dv}}s\quad\neg\mathsf{abs}(t)$}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=65.78745pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 14.92825pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{t}\,{u}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dv}}{s}\,{u}$}}}}\\
\\
{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dv}}s\quad u\in\mathsf{ne}_{\mathtt{v}}\cup\mathsf{vr}_{\mathtt{v}}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=81.75078pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 22.90991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{u}\,{t}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dv}}{u}\,{s}$}}}}\quad{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 2.06483pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dv}}s\quad\neg\mathsf{val}(t)$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=67.91713pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{u}[{x}\backslash{t}]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dv}}{u}[{x}\backslash{s}]$}}}}\qquad{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 2.5179pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dv}}s\quad u\in\mathsf{ne}_{\mathtt{v}}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=67.91711pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{t}[{x}\backslash{u}]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dv}}{s}[{x}\backslash{u}]$}}}}\end{array}

As a matter of notation, for
\mathcal{X}\in\{{\mathtt{dn},\mathtt{dv}}\}
, we write
t\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathcal{X}}^{(\mathit{m},\mathit{e})}u
if
t\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathcal{X}}u
using
\mathit{m}

multiplicative steps and
\mathit{e}
exponential steps.

The normal forms of the non-deterministic and the deterministic versions of
CBN/CBV are the same, in turn characterised by the grammars

\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{n}}
/
\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{v}}

[
12
]
.

Proposition 1
.

Let
t\in\mathcal{T_{\lambda}}
. Then,
t\not\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}}
iff
t\not\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}
iff
t\in\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{n}}
and
t\not\rightarrow_{\mathtt{v}}
iff

t\not\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dv}}
iff
t\in\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{v}}
.

Proof.

1.

t\not\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}}
implies
t\not\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}
follows from

\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}\subset\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}}
.

2.

t\in\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{n}}
iff
t\not\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}}
is shown
simultaneously by considering the following statements:

(a)

t\in\mathsf{ne}_{\mathtt{n}}
iff
t\not\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}}

and
\neg\mathsf{abs}(t)
.

(b)

t\in\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{n}}
iff
t\not\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}}
.

(a)

The left-to-right implication is
straightforward. For the right-to-left implication we reason by induction
on terms. Suppose
t\not\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}}
and
\neg\mathsf{abs}(t)
. If

t
is a substitution, then rule
\mathtt{sn}
is applicable, contradiction.
Then
t={u}\,{u^{\prime}}
, where
\neg\mathsf{abs}(u)
(otherwise
\mathtt{dB}
would
be applicable) and
u\not\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}}
(otherwise
t
would be

\mathtt{n}
-reducible). The
i.h.
(
2a
) gives
u\in\mathsf{ne}_{\mathtt{n}}

and thus we conclude
t={u}\,{u^{\prime}}\in\mathsf{ne}_{\mathtt{n}}
.

(b)

The left-to-right implication is straightforward. For the
right-to-left implication we reason by induction on terms. If
t=\lambda{x}.{u}
, then
u\not\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}}
, and the
i.h.
(
2b
) gives
u\in\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{n}}
, which implies in turn

\lambda{x}.{u}\in\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{n}}
. Otherwise, we apply the previous case.

3.

t\not\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}
implies
t\in\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{n}}
follows a straightforward
adaptation of the previous right-to-left implication.

4.

t\not\rightarrow_{\mathtt{v}}
implies
t\not\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{dv}}
follows from

\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dv}}\subset\rightarrow_{\mathtt{v}}
.

5.

t\in\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{v}}
iff
t\not\rightarrow_{\mathtt{v}}
is shown
simultaneously by considering the following statements:

(a)

t\in\mathsf{vr}_{\mathtt{v}}
iff
t\not\rightarrow_{\mathtt{v}}
and
\neg\mathsf{abs}(t)
and
\neg\mathsf{app}(t)
.

(b)

t\in\mathsf{ne}_{\mathtt{v}}
iff
t\not\rightarrow_{\mathtt{v}}
and
\neg\mathsf{abs}(t)
and
\neg\mathsf{var}(t)
.

(c)

t\in\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{v}}
iff
t\not\rightarrow_{\mathtt{v}}
.

(a)

The left-to-right implication is straightforward. For the
right-to-left implication we reason by induction on terms. Suppose

t\not\rightarrow_{\mathtt{v}}
and
\neg\mathsf{abs}(t)
and
\neg\mathsf{app}(t)
.
Then necessarily
\mathsf{var}(t)
. If
t={u}[{x}\backslash{u^{\prime}}]
, then
u,u^{\prime}\not\rightarrow_{\mathtt{v}}
(otherwise
t
would be

\mathtt{v}
-reducible),
\neg\mathsf{abs}(u^{\prime})
and
\neg\mathsf{var}(u^{\prime})
(otherwise

t
would be
\mathtt{sv}
-reducible),
\neg\mathsf{abs}(u)
and
\neg\mathsf{app}(u)
. The
i.h.
(
5a
) gives
u\in\mathsf{vr}_{\mathtt{v}}
and
u^{\prime}\in\mathsf{ne}_{\mathtt{v}}
, so that
we conclude
t\in\mathsf{vr}_{\mathtt{v}}
. If
t=x
we trivially conclude
t\in\mathsf{vr}_{\mathtt{v}}
.

(b)

The left-to-right implication is straightforward. For the
right-to-left implication we reason by induction on terms. Suppose
t\not\rightarrow_{\mathtt{v}}
and
\neg\mathsf{abs}(t)
and
\neg\mathsf{var}(t)
. Then
necessarily
\mathsf{app}(t)
. If
t={u}[{x}\backslash{u^{\prime}}]
, then
u,u^{\prime}\not\rightarrow_{\mathtt{v}}
(otherwise
t
would be

\mathtt{v}
-reducible),
\neg\mathsf{abs}(u^{\prime})
and
\neg\mathsf{var}(u^{\prime})
(otherwise

t
would be
\mathtt{sv}
-reducible),
\neg\mathsf{abs}(u)
and
\neg\mathsf{var}(u)
. The
i.h.
(
5b
) gives
u\in\mathsf{ne}_{\mathtt{v}}
and
u^{\prime}\in\mathsf{ne}_{\mathtt{v}}
, so that we
conclude
t\in\mathsf{ne}_{\mathtt{v}}
. If
t={u}\,{u^{\prime}}
, then
u,u^{\prime}\not\rightarrow_{\mathtt{v}}
(otherwise
t
would be

\mathtt{v}
-reducible),
\neg\mathsf{abs}(u)
(otherwise
t
would be

\mathtt{dB}
-reducible). Moreover,
\neg\mathsf{app}(u)
or
\neg\mathsf{var}(u)
holds. The

i.h.
(
5c
) gives
u^{\prime}\in\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{v}}
and (
u\in\mathsf{vr}_{\mathtt{v}}
or

u\in\mathsf{ne}_{\mathtt{v}}
). In both cases we conclude
t={u}\,{u^{\prime}}\in\mathsf{ne}_{\mathtt{v}}
.

(c)

The left-to-right implication is straightforward. For the
right-to-left implication we reason by induction on terms. Suppose
t\not\rightarrow_{\mathtt{v}}
. If
t=\lambda{x}.{u}
, then
t\in\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{v}}

is straightforward. If
t={u}[{x}\backslash{u^{\prime}}]
, then
u,u^{\prime}\not\rightarrow_{\mathtt{v}}
(otherwise
t
would be

\mathtt{v}
-reducible),
\neg\mathsf{abs}(u^{\prime})
and
\neg\mathsf{var}(u^{\prime})
(otherwise

t
would be
\mathtt{sv}
-reducible). The
i.h.
(
5c
) and
(
5b
) give
u\in\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{v}}
and
u^{\prime}\in\mathsf{ne}_{\mathtt{v}}
, so that we
conclude
t\in\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{v}}
. If
t={u}\,{u^{\prime}}
, then
u\not\rightarrow_{\mathtt{v}}
and
u^{\prime}\not\rightarrow_{\mathtt{v}}
. The
i.h.
(
5c
) on
u^{\prime}
gives
u^{\prime}\in\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{v}}
. Moreover,

\neg\mathsf{abs}(u)
, otherwise
t
would be
\mathtt{v}
-reducible. Then

\neg\mathsf{var}(u)
or
\neg\mathsf{app}(u)
. The
i.h.
(
5a
) or
(
5b
) gives
u\in\mathsf{vr}_{\mathtt{v}}
or
u\in\mathsf{ne}_{\mathtt{v}}
. Thus

{u}\,{u^{\prime}}\in\mathsf{ne}_{\mathtt{v}}\subseteq\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{v}}
as required.

6.

t\not\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dv}}
implies
t\in\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{v}}
follows a straightforward
adaptation of the previous right-to-left implication.

∎

(Head) CBN ignores reduction inside arguments of applications, while (Open) CBV
ignores reduction inside abstractions, then CBN (resp. CBV) normal forms are
measured by the following
\mathtt{n}
-size
(resp.

\mathtt{v}
-size
) function:

\begin{array}[]{llllll}|{x}|_{\mathtt{n}}:=0&|{\lambda{x}.{t}}|_{\mathtt{n}}:=|{t}|_{\mathtt{n}}+1&|{{t}\,{u}}|_{\mathtt{n}}:=|{t}|_{\mathtt{n}}+1&|{{t}[{x}\backslash{u}]}|_{\mathtt{n}}:=|{t}|_{\mathtt{n}}\\
|{x}|_{\mathtt{v}}:=0&|{\lambda{x}.{t}}|_{\mathtt{v}}:=0&|{{t}\,{u}}|_{\mathtt{v}}:=|{t}|_{\mathtt{v}}+|{u}|_{\mathtt{v}}+1&|{{t}[{x}\backslash{u}]}|_{\mathtt{v}}:=|{t}|_{\mathtt{v}}+|{u}|_{\mathtt{v}}\end{array}

3
Tight Call-by-Name

We now introduce a tight type system
\mathcal{N}
for CBN which captures
independent exact measures for
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}}
-reduction sequences.
This result is not surprising, since it extends the one in
[
1
]

from the pure
\lambda
-calculus to our CBN calculus. However, we revisit the
op.cit. approach by appropriately splitting reduction into multiplicative and
exponential steps, a reformulation necessary to establish a
precise correspondence with the tight type system for the
\lambda!
-calculus.
In particular, our
counting
mechanism slightly differs
from
[
1
]
(details below).

In system
\mathcal{N}
there are two base types:

\mathtt{a}
types terms whose normal form is an abstraction, and

\mathtt{n}
types terms whose normal form is CBN neutral. The grammar of
types is given by:

(Tight Types)

\mathtt{tt}

::=

\mathtt{n}\mid\mathtt{a}

(Types)

\sigma,\tau

::=

\mathtt{tt}\mid\mathcal{M}\mid{\mathcal{M}}\to{\sigma}

(Multitypes)

\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}

::=

[\sigma_{i}]_{i\in I}
where
I
is a finite set

Multitypes are multisets of types. The
empty multitype
is denoted by

[\,]
,
\sqcup
denotes multitype union, and
\sqsubseteq
multitype
inclusion. Also,
|\mathcal{M}|
denotes the size of the multitype, thus if
\mathcal{M}=[\sigma_{i}]_{i\in I}
then
|\mathcal{M}|=\#(I)
.
Notice that the grammar for types slightly differs from
[
1
]
,
in particular types are now allowed to be just multitypes. The main reason to
adopt this change is that this (unique) grammar is used for our three
formalisms CBN, CBV, and CBPV, changing only the definition of tight types for
each case
2
2

2

In the intersection type literature CBN and CBV do always
adopt different grammars.
.

Typing contexts
(or just
contexts
), written
\Gamma,\Delta
, are functions from variables to multitypes, assigning the empty
multitype to all but a finite set of variables. The domain of
\Gamma
is given
by
\mathtt{dom}({\Gamma})\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textit{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}$}}\{{x\mid\Gamma(x)\neq[\,]}\}
. The
union
of contexts
, written
{\Gamma}\mathrel{+}{\Delta}
, is defined by

({\Gamma}\mathrel{+}{\Delta})(x)\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textit{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}$}}\Gamma(x)\sqcup\Delta(x)
. An example
is
{({x}:{[\sigma]},{y}:{[\tau]})}\mathrel{+}{({x}:{[\sigma]},{z}:{[\tau]})}=({x}:{[\sigma,\sigma]},{y}:{[\tau]},{z}:{[\tau]})
. This notion
is extended to several contexts as expected, so that
{}\mathrel{+_{i\in I}}{}\Gamma_{i}
denotes a finite union of contexts (particularly the empty context
when
I=\emptyset
). We write
{\Gamma}\mathrel{\setminus\!\!\setminus}{x}
for the context

({\Gamma}\mathrel{\setminus\!\!\setminus}{x})(x)=[\,]
and
({\Gamma}\mathrel{\setminus\!\!\setminus}{x})(y)=\Gamma(y)
if
y\neq x
.

Type judgements
have the form

{\Gamma}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}},{\mathit{e}},{\mathit{s}})}{{t}:{\sigma}}
, where
\Gamma
is
a typing context,
t
is a term,
\sigma
is a type, and the
counters

(\mathit{m},\mathit{e},\mathit{s})
are expected to provide the following information:

\mathit{m}
(resp.
\mathit{e}
) indicates the number of multiplicative
\mathtt{m}
-steps
(resp. exponential
\mathtt{e}
-steps) to normal form, while
\mathit{s}
indicates
the
\mathtt{n}
-size of this normal form. It is worth noticing that the

\lambda
-calculus hides both multiplicative and
exponential steps in one single
\beta
-reduction rule
[
1
]
, so
that only two counters suffice, one for the number of
\beta
-reduction steps,
and another for the size of normal forms. Here we want to discriminate between
multiplicative/exponential steps, in the sense that the execution of an ES
generates an exponential step, but not a multiplicative one. This becomes
possible due to the CBN/CBV alternative specifications with ES that we have
adopted, and that is why we need three independent counters, in contrast to the

\lambda
-calculus.

The type system
\mathcal{N}
for CBN is given in
Fig.
1
(
persistent
rules)
and
2
(
consuming
rules). A constructor
is consuming (resp. persistent) if it is consumed (resp. not consumed) during

\mathtt{n}
-reduction. For instance, in

{{{\tt K}}\,{{\tt I}}}\,{\Omega}
the two abstractions of
{\tt K}
are
consuming, while the abstraction of
{\tt I}
is persistent, and all the other
constructors are also consuming, except those of
\Omega
that turns out to be
an untyped subterm. The persistent rules
(Fig.
1
) are those typing persistent
constructors, so that none of them increases the first two counters, but only
possibly the third one, which contributes to the size of the normal form. The
consuming rules (Fig.
2
), in contrast, type
consuming constructors, so that they may increase the first two counters,
contributing to the length of the normalisation sequence. Notice in particular
that there are only two rules contributing to the multiplicative/exponential
counting:

(1)

rule
\mathtt{({app^{\mathcal{N}}_{c}})}
types a
consuming
application, meaning that
its left-hand side subterm reduces to an abstraction, then causing a
multiplicative step (rule
\mathtt{dB}
)
3
3

3

In both
[
1
]

and
[
36
]
, it is the consuming abstraction which contributes to
the multiplicative steps.
followed later by an exponential step; while

(2)

rule
\mathtt{({es^{\mathcal{N}}_{c}})}
types a
consuming
substitution causing an
exponential step. In both cases, it is the constructor
application/substitution which is considered to be consumed, without any
further hypothesis on the form of the subterm that appears inside this
consuming constructor (recall that any term can be substituted in CBN).
This phenomenon facilitates in particular the identification of exponential
steps in CBN, in contrast to CBV and CBPV.

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 8.75812pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty{\Gamma}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}},{\mathit{e}},{\mathit{s}})}{{t}:{\mathtt{n}}}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=66.69418pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{({app^{\mathcal{N}}_{p}})}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\Gamma}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}},{\mathit{e}},{\mathit{s}+1})}{{{t}\,{u}}:{\mathtt{n}}}$}}}}\qquad{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty{\Gamma}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}},{\mathit{e}},{\mathit{s}})}{{t}:{\mathtt{tt}}}\quad\mathsf{tight}(\Gamma(x))$}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=111.72661pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{({abs_{p}})}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 8.9687pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{{\Gamma}\mathrel{\setminus\!\!\setminus}{x}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}},{\mathit{e}},{\mathit{s}+1})}{{\lambda{x}.{t}}:{\mathtt{a}}}$}}}}\end{array}

Figure 1:
System
\mathcal{N}
for the Call-by-Name Calculus: Persistent Typing Rules.

\kern-20.00003pt\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 28.53363pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vphantom{\Gamma}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=63.31728pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{({var_{c}})}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{{x}:{[\sigma]}}\vdash^{({0},{0},{0})}{{x}:{\sigma}}$}}}}\qquad{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 29.61465pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty{\Gamma}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}},{\mathit{e}},{\mathit{s}})}{{t}:{\tau}}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=107.52884pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{({abs_{c}})}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{{\Gamma}\mathrel{\setminus\!\!\setminus}{x}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}},{\mathit{e}},{\mathit{s}})}{{\lambda{x}.{t}}:{{\Gamma(x)}\to{\tau}}}$}}}}\\
\\
{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty{\Gamma}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}},{\mathit{e}},{\mathit{s}})}{{t}:{{[\sigma_{i}]_{i\in I}}\to{\tau}}}\quad({{\Delta_{i}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}_{i}},{\mathit{e}_{i}},{\mathit{s}_{i}})}{{u}:{\sigma_{i}}}})_{i\in I}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=181.29018pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{({app^{\mathcal{N}}_{c}})}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 6.59761pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{{\Gamma}\mathrel{+}{\Delta}}\vdash^{({1+\mathit{m}+_{i\in I}{\mathit{m}_{i}}},{1+\mathit{e}+_{i\in I}{\mathit{e}_{i}}},{\mathit{s}+_{i\in I}{\mathit{s}_{i}}})}{{{t}\,{u}}:{\tau}}$}}}}\\
\\
{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 19.54321pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty{\Gamma;{x}:{[\sigma_{i}]_{i\in I}}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}},{\mathit{e}},{\mathit{s}})}{{t}:{\tau}}\kern 5.0pt({{\Delta_{i}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}_{i}},{\mathit{e}_{i}},{\mathit{s}_{i}})}{{u}:{\sigma_{i}}}})_{i\in I}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=216.64742pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{({es^{\mathcal{N}}_{c}})}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{{({\Gamma}\mathrel{\setminus\!\!\setminus}{x})}\mathrel{+_{i\in I}}{\Delta_{i}}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}+_{i\in I}{\mathit{m}_{i}}},{1+\mathit{e}+_{i\in I}{\mathit{e}_{i}}},{\mathit{s}+_{i\in I}{\mathit{s}_{i}}})}{{{t}[{x}\backslash{u}]}:{\tau}}$}}}}\end{array}

Figure 2:
System
\mathcal{N}
for the Call-by-Name Calculus: Consuming Typing Rules.

This dichotomy between consuming/persistent constructors has been first used
in
[
36
]
for the
\lambda
and
\lambda\mu
-calculi, and adapted
here for our distant versions of CBN/CBV as well as for the

\lambda!
-calculus. We write

{}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Gamma}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}},{\mathit{e}},{\mathit{s}})}{{t}:{\sigma}}}

if there is a
(tree) type derivation
of the judgement

{\Gamma}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}},{\mathit{e}},{\mathit{s}})}{{t}:{\sigma}}
in system

\mathcal{N}
. The term
t
is typable in system
\mathcal{N}
, or

\mathcal{N}
-typable, iff there is a context
\Gamma
, a type
\sigma
and
counters
(\mathit{m},\mathit{e},\mathit{s})
such that

{}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Gamma}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}},{\mathit{e}},{\mathit{s}})}{{t}:{\sigma}}}
.
We use the capital Greek letters
\Phi,\Psi,\ldots
to name type derivations,
by writing for example

{\Phi}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Gamma}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}},{\mathit{e}},{\mathit{s}})}{{t}:{\sigma}}}
.
As (local) counters of judgements in a given derivation
\Phi
contribute to
the global counters of the derivation itself, there is an alternative way to
define counters associated to a derivation
\Phi
: the first counter

\mathit{m}_{\Phi}
is given by the number of rules
\mathtt{({app^{\mathcal{N}}_{c}})}
in
\Phi
, the
second counter
\mathit{e}_{\Phi}
is the number of rules
\mathtt{({es^{\mathcal{N}}_{c}})}
in
\Phi

and finally the third counter
\mathit{s}_{\Phi}
is the number of rules

\mathtt{({app^{\mathcal{N}}_{p}})}
and
\mathtt{({abs_{p}})}
in
\Phi
. We prefer however to
systematically write counters in judgements to easy the understanding of the
examples and proofs.

A
multitype

[\sigma_{i}]_{i\in I}
is
tight
,
written
\mathsf{tight}([\sigma_{i}]_{i\in I})
, if
\sigma_{i}\in\mathtt{tt}

for all
i\in I
. A
context

\Gamma
is said to be
tight

if it assigns tight multitypes to all variables. A
type derivation

{\Phi}\rhd_{\mathcal{B}}{{\Gamma}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}},{\mathit{e}},{\mathit{s}})}{{t}:{\sigma}}}

is
tight
if
\Gamma
is tight and
\sigma\in\mathtt{tt}
.

The proofs of soundness and completeness related to our CBN type system are
respectively based on subject reduction and expansion properties, and they are
very similar to those in
[
1
]
. The most important point to be
mentioned is that system
\mathcal{N}
is now counting
separately
the
multiplicative and exponential steps of
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}}
-reductions to
normal-form.

Soundness.

The soundness property is based on a series of auxiliary results.

Lemma 2

(Tight Spreading)
.

Let

{\Phi}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Gamma}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}},{\mathit{e}},{\mathit{s}})}{{t}:{\sigma}}}

such that
\Gamma
is tight.
If
t\in\mathsf{ne}_{\mathtt{n}}
, then
\sigma\in\mathtt{tt}
.

Proof.

By induction on
\Phi
.

•

\mathtt{({app^{\mathcal{N}}_{p}})}
. Then
\sigma\in\mathtt{tt}
by definition.

•

\mathtt{({abs_{p}})}
. This case does not apply since
t\notin\mathsf{ne}_{\mathtt{n}}
.

•

\mathtt{({var_{c}})}
. This case is immediate since
\Gamma=[\sigma]

tight implies
\sigma\in\mathtt{tt}
.

•

\mathtt{({app^{\mathcal{N}}_{c}})}
. Then
t={r}\,{u}
. If
t\in\mathsf{ne}_{\mathtt{n}}
, then
r\in\mathsf{ne}_{\mathtt{n}}
. We have a derivation

{\Phi_{r}}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Gamma^{\prime}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}^{\prime}},{\mathit{e}^{\prime}},{\mathit{s}^{\prime}})}{{r}:{[{\mathcal{M}}\to{\sigma}]}}}
,
with
\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma
, so that
\Gamma^{\prime}
is also tight. The
i.h.
gives

[{\mathcal{M}}\to{\sigma}]\in\mathtt{tt}
, and this is a
contradiction. Hence, this case does not apply.

•

\mathtt{({abs_{c}})}
and
\mathtt{({es^{\mathcal{N}}_{c}})}
. These cases do not apply since
t\notin\mathsf{ne}_{\mathtt{n}}
.

∎

Lemma 3
.

Let

{\Phi}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Gamma}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}},{\mathit{e}},{\mathit{s}})}{{t}:{\sigma}}}

tight. Then,
\mathit{m}=\mathit{e}=0
iff
t\in\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{n}}
.

Proof.

\left.\Rightarrow\right)
We prove simultaneously the following statements by
induction on
\Phi
.

1.

{\Phi}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Gamma}\vdash^{({0},{0},{\mathit{s}})}{{t}:{\sigma}}}

tight and
\mathsf{var}(t)
or
\mathsf{app}(t)
implies
t\in\mathsf{ne}_{\mathtt{n}}
.

2.

{\Phi}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Gamma}\vdash^{({0},{0},{\mathit{s}})}{{t}:{\sigma}}}

tight and
\mathsf{abs}(t)
implies
t\in\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{n}}
.

•

\mathtt{({var_{c}})}
. Then
t=x
and
\mathsf{var}(t)
. By definition
x\in\mathsf{ne}_{\mathtt{v}}
trivially holds.

•

\mathtt{({app^{\mathcal{N}}_{p}})}
. Then
t={r}\,{u}
, and

{\Gamma}\vdash^{({0},{0},{\mathit{s}-1})}{{r}:{\mathtt{n}}}
is also tight.
We are in the case
\mathsf{app}(t)
so we need to show
t\in\mathsf{ne}_{\mathtt{n}}
. The
i.h.
(
1
) gives
r\in\mathsf{ne}_{\mathtt{n}}
and we thus conclude

{r}\,{u}\in\mathsf{ne}_{\mathtt{n}}
.

•

\mathtt{({abs_{p}})}
. Then
t=\lambda{x}.{r}
, and the derivation typing

r
is also tight and has the two first counters equal to
0
. The
i.h.
gives

r\in\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{n}}
, then we conclude
t\in\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{n}}
.

•

\mathtt{({app^{\mathcal{N}}_{c}})}
,
\mathtt{({es^{\mathcal{N}}_{c}})}
. The hypothesis about the counters is
not verified so these cases do not apply.

•

\mathtt{({abs_{c}})}
. This case does not apply since the derivation cannot
be tight.

\left.\Leftarrow\right)
By induction on
t
.

•

t=x
. Then,
\Phi
necessarily ends with rule
\mathtt{({var_{c}})}
and the
statement trivially holds.

•

t={r}\,{u}
. By definition
t\in\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{n}}
gives
r\in\mathsf{ne}_{\mathtt{n}}
. There are two cases to consider:

1.

if
\Phi
ends with rule
\mathtt{({app^{\mathcal{N}}_{p}})}
, then
\sigma=\mathtt{n}
, and

{\Gamma}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}},{\mathit{e}},{\mathit{s}})}{{r}:{\mathtt{n}}}
. The
i.h.
gives
\mathit{m}=\mathit{e}=0
and we are done.

2.

if
\Phi
ends with rule
\mathtt{({app^{\mathcal{N}}_{c}})}
, then

{\Phi_{r}}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Gamma^{\prime}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}^{\prime}},{\mathit{e}^{\prime}},{\mathit{s}^{\prime}})}{{r}:{[{\mathcal{M}}\to{\tau}]}}}

where
\Gamma^{\prime}
is tight. Moreover, Lem.
2
gives

[{\mathcal{M}}\to{\tau}]\in\mathtt{tt}
which is a contradiction.
Hence, this case does not apply.

•

t=\lambda{x}.{r}
. Then
r\in\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{n}}
and there are two cases to
consider for
\Phi
:

1.

if
\Phi
ends with rule
\mathtt{({abs_{p}})}
, then the subderivation of

\Phi
is tight, and thus property holds by the
i.h.

2.

if
\Phi
ends with rule
\mathtt{({abs_{c}})}
, then
\Phi
cannot be tight
so this case does not apply.

•

t={r}[{x}\backslash{u}]
. By definition
t\notin\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{n}}
so this cases
does not apply.

∎

Lemma 4
.

Let

{\Phi}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Gamma}\vdash^{({0},{0},{\mathit{s}})}{{t}:{\sigma}}}
.
If
\Phi
is tight and
t\in\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{n}}
, then
\mathit{s}=|{t}|_{\mathtt{n}}
.

Proof.

By induction on
\Phi
.

•

\mathtt{({var_{c}})}
. Then
t=x
and
\mathit{s}=0=|{t}|_{\mathtt{n}}
as expected.

•

\mathtt{({app^{\mathcal{N}}_{p}})}
. Then
t={r}\,{u}
,
\sigma=\mathtt{n}
,
and
{\Gamma}\vdash^{({0},{0},{\mathit{s}-1})}{{r}:{\mathtt{n}}}
. The
i.h.
gives

\mathit{s}-1=|{r}|_{\mathtt{n}}
and hence
\mathit{s}=|{r}|_{\mathtt{n}}+1=|{t}|_{\mathtt{n}}
.

•

\mathtt{({abs_{p}})}
. Then
t=\lambda{x}.{r}
, and the subderivation
typing
r
is tight and has counter
\mathit{s}-1
. The
i.h.
gives
\mathit{s}-1=|{r}|_{\mathtt{n}}
and hence
\mathit{s}=|{r}|_{\mathtt{n}}+1=|{t}|_{\mathtt{n}}
.

•

\mathtt{({app^{\mathcal{N}}_{c}})}
. Then first two counters are not
0
so this case does
not apply.

•

\mathtt{({abs_{c}})}
. Then
\Phi
is not tight so this case does not apply.

•

\mathtt{({es^{\mathcal{N}}_{c}})}
. Then the second counter is not
0
so this cases does
not apply.

∎

Lemma 5

(Substitution)
.

Let

{\Phi_{t}}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Gamma;x:[\sigma_{i}]_{i\in I}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}},{\mathit{e}},{\mathit{s}})}{{t}:{\tau}}}

and

({\Phi_{u}}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Delta_{i}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}_{i}},{\mathit{e}_{i}},{\mathit{s}_{i}})}{{u}:{\sigma_{i}}}})_{i\in I}
.
Then,

{\Phi_{{{t}\left\{{x}\backslash{u}\right\}}}}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{{\Gamma}\mathrel{+_{i\in I}}{\Delta_{i}}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}+_{i\in I}\mathit{m}_{i}},{\mathit{e}+_{i\in I}\mathit{e}},{\mathit{s}+_{i\in I}\mathit{s}_{i}})}{{{{t}\left\{{x}\backslash{u}\right\}}}:{\tau}}}
.

Proof.

By induction on
\Phi_{t}
.

•

\mathtt{({var_{c}})}
. There are two cases.

–

t=x
. Then
{{t}\left\{{x}\backslash{u}\right\}}=u
and
|I|=1
. More
precisely,

{\Phi_{t}}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{{x}:{[\sigma_{1}]}}\vdash^{({0},{0},{0})}{{x}:{\sigma_{1}}}}
.
We conclude by taking
\Phi_{{{t}\left\{{x}\backslash{u}\right\}}}=\Phi^{1}_{u}
since the
counters are as expected.

–

t=y\neq x
. Then,
{{t}\left\{{x}\backslash{u}\right\}}=y
and
|I|=0
. More
precisely, we have

{\Phi_{t}}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{{y}:{[\tau]}}\vdash^{({0},{0},{0})}{{y}:{\tau}}}
.
Hence we conclude by taking
\Phi_{{{t}\left\{{x}\backslash{u}\right\}}}=\Phi_{t}
and
the counters are as expected.

•

\mathtt{({app^{\mathcal{N}}_{p}})}
. Then
t={r}\,{s}
,
{{t}\left\{{x}\backslash{s}\right\}}={{{r}\left\{{x}\backslash{v}\right\}}}\,{{{s}\left\{{x}\backslash{v}\right\}}}
and
\tau=\mathtt{n}
. We have a premise

{\Phi_{r}}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Gamma;x:[\sigma_{i}]_{i\in I}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}},{\mathit{e}},{\mathit{s}-1})}{{r}:{\mathtt{n}}}}
.
By the
i.h.
we get a type derivation

{\Phi_{{{r}\left\{{x}\backslash{u}\right\}}}}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{{\Gamma}\mathrel{+_{i\in I}}{\Delta_{i}}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}+_{i\in I}\mathit{m}_{i}},{\mathit{e}+_{i\in I}\mathit{e}},{\mathit{s}-1+_{i\in I}\mathit{s}_{i}})}{{{{r}\left\{{x}\backslash{u}\right\}}}:{\mathtt{n}}}}
.
We conclude by applying
\mathtt{({app^{\mathcal{N}}_{p}})}
to this premise, thus obtaining

{\Phi_{{{t}\left\{{x}\backslash{u}\right\}}}}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{{\Gamma}\mathrel{+_{i\in I}}{\Delta_{i}}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}+_{i\in I}\mathit{m}_{i}},{\mathit{e}+_{i\in I}\mathit{e}},{\mathit{s}+_{i\in I}\mathit{s}_{i}})}{{{{t}\left\{{x}\backslash{u}\right\}}}:{\mathtt{n}}}}
as required.

•

All the other cases are straightforward by induction.

∎

Lemma 6

(Exact Subject Reduction)
.

Let

{\Phi}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Gamma}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}},{\mathit{e}},{\mathit{s}})}{{t}:{\sigma}}}

be a tight derivation. If
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}t^{\prime}
, then there is

{\Phi^{\prime}}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Gamma}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}^{\prime}},{\mathit{e}^{\prime}},{\mathit{s}})}{{t^{\prime}}:{\sigma}}}

such that

1.

\mathit{m}^{\prime}=\mathit{m}-1
and

\mathit{e}^{\prime}=\mathit{e}
if
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}t^{\prime}
is an
\mathtt{m}
-step.

2.

\mathit{e}^{\prime}=\mathit{e}-1
and

\mathit{m}^{\prime}=\mathit{m}
if
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}t^{\prime}
is an
\mathtt{e}
-step.

Proof.

We actually prove the following stronger statement that allows us to reason
inductively:

Let
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}t^{\prime}
and

{\Phi}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Gamma}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}},{\mathit{e}},{\mathit{s}})}{{t}:{\sigma}}}

such that
\Gamma
is tight, and either
\sigma\in\mathtt{tt}
or

\neg\mathsf{abs}(t)
. Then, there exists

{\Phi^{\prime}}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Gamma}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}^{\prime}},{\mathit{e}^{\prime}},{\mathit{s}})}{{t^{\prime}}:{\sigma}}}

such that

1.

\mathit{m}^{\prime}=\mathit{m}-1
and
\mathit{e}^{\prime}=\mathit{e}
if
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}t^{\prime}
is an

\mathtt{m}
-step.

2.

\mathit{e}^{\prime}=\mathit{e}-1
and
\mathit{m}^{\prime}=\mathit{m}
if
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}t^{\prime}
is an

\mathtt{e}
-step.

We proceed by induction on
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}t^{\prime}
.

•

t={{\mathtt{L}}\langle{\lambda{x}.{r}}\rangle}\,{u}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}{\mathtt{L}}\langle{{r}[{x}\backslash{u}]}\rangle=t^{\prime}
is an
\mathtt{m}
-step.
We proceed by induction on
\mathtt{L}
. We only show here the case
\mathtt{L}=\Box
as the inductive case is straightforward.

We first remark that
\Phi
cannot end with rule
\mathtt{({app_{p}})}
since

\lambda{x}.{u}
cannot be typed with
\mathtt{n}
, then
\Phi
ends with

\mathtt{({app^{\mathcal{N}}_{c}})}
and has the following form:

\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty{\Phi_{r}}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Gamma^{\prime};{x}:{[\sigma_{i}]_{i\in I}}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}_{1}},{\mathit{e}_{1}},{\mathit{s}_{1}})}{{r}:{\sigma}}}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=129.84175pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{({abs_{c}})}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 8.88776pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\Gamma^{\prime}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}_{1}},{\mathit{e}_{1}},{\mathit{s}_{1}})}{{\lambda{x}.{r}}:{{[\sigma_{i}]_{i\in I}}\to{\sigma}}}$}}}}\enskip\penalty\quad({\Phi^{i}_{u}}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Delta_{i}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}^{i}_{2}},{\mathit{e}^{i}_{2}},{\mathit{s}^{i}_{2}})}{{u}:{\sigma_{i}}}})_{i\in I}

\mathtt{({app^{\mathcal{N}}_{c}})}

\displaystyle{{\Gamma^{\prime}}\mathrel{+_{i\in I}}{\Delta_{i}}}\vdash^{({1+\mathit{m}_{1}+_{i\in I}{\mathit{m}^{i}_{2}}},{1+\mathit{e}_{1}+_{i\in I}{\mathit{e}^{i}_{2}}},{\mathit{s}_{1}+_{i\in I}{\mathit{s}^{i}_{2}}})}{{{(\lambda{x}.{r})}\,{u}}:{\sigma}}

with
\mathit{m}=1+\mathit{m}_{1}+_{i\in I}{\mathit{m}^{i}_{2}}
,
\mathit{e}=1+\mathit{e}_{1}+_{i\in I}{\mathit{e}^{i}_{2}}
and
\mathit{s}=\mathit{s}_{1}+_{i\in I}{\mathit{s}^{i}_{2}}
. We conclude
by
\mathtt{({es^{\mathcal{N}}_{c}})}
with the following type derivation

\displaystyle\penalty{\Phi_{r}}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Gamma^{\prime};{x}:{[\sigma_{i}]_{i\in I}}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}_{1}},{\mathit{e}_{1}},{\mathit{s}_{1}})}{{r}:{\sigma}}}\quad({\Phi^{i}_{u}}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Delta_{i}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}^{i}_{2}},{\mathit{e}^{i}_{2}},{\mathit{s}^{i}_{2}})}{{u}:{\sigma_{i}}}})_{i\in I}

\mathtt{({es^{\mathcal{N}}_{c}})}

\displaystyle{\Phi^{\prime}}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{{\Gamma^{\prime}}\mathrel{+_{i\in I}}{\Delta_{i}}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}_{1}+_{i\in I}{\mathit{m}^{i}_{2}}},{1+\mathit{e}_{1}+_{i\in I}{\mathit{e}^{i}_{2}}},{\mathit{s}_{1}+_{i\in I}{\mathit{s}^{i}_{2}}})}{{{r}[{x}\backslash{u}]}:{\sigma}}}

taking
\mathit{m}^{\prime}=\mathit{m}_{1}+_{i\in I}{\mathit{m}^{i}_{2}}=\mathit{m}-1
and
\mathit{e}^{\prime}=1+\mathit{e}_{1}+_{i\in I}{\mathit{e}^{i}_{2}}=\mathit{e}
.

•

t={r}[{x}\backslash{u}]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}{{r}\left\{{x}\backslash{u}\right\}}=t^{\prime}
is an

\mathtt{e}
-step. Then, rule
\mathtt{({es^{\mathcal{N}}_{c}})}
is necessarily used

\displaystyle\penalty{\Phi_{r}}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Gamma^{\prime};{x}:{[\sigma_{i}]_{i\in I}}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}_{1}},{\mathit{e}_{1}},{\mathit{s}_{1}})}{{r}:{\sigma}}}\quad({\Phi^{i}_{u}}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Delta_{i}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}^{i}_{2}},{\mathit{e}^{i}_{2}},{\mathit{s}^{i}_{2}})}{{u}:{\sigma_{i}}}})_{i\in I}

\mathtt{({es^{\mathcal{N}}_{c}})}

\displaystyle{\Phi}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{{\Gamma^{\prime}}\mathrel{+_{i\in I}}{\Delta_{i}}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}_{1}+_{i\in I}\mathit{m}^{i}_{2}},{1+\mathit{e}_{1}+_{i\in I}\mathit{e}^{i}_{2}},{\mathit{s}_{1}+_{i\in I}\mathit{s}^{i}_{2}})}{{{r}[{x}\backslash{u}]}:{\sigma}}}

with
\mathit{m}=\mathit{m}_{1}+_{i\in I}\mathit{m}^{i}_{2}
,
\mathit{e}=1+\mathit{e}_{1}+_{i\in I}\mathit{e}^{i}_{2}
and
\mathit{s}=\mathit{s}_{1}+_{i\in I}\mathit{s}^{i}_{2}
. We conclude by
applying Lem.
5
with
\Phi_{r}
and
\Phi_{u}
,
thus obtaining

{\Phi^{\prime}}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{{\Gamma^{\prime}}\mathrel{+_{i\in I}}{\Delta_{i}}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}_{1}+_{i\in I}\mathit{m}^{i}_{2}},{\mathit{e}_{1}+_{i\in I}\mathit{e}^{i}_{2}},{\mathit{s}_{1}+_{i\in I}\mathit{s}^{i}_{2}})}{{{{t}\left\{{x}\backslash{u}\right\}}}:{\sigma}}}
.
Note that the counters are as expected.

•

t={r}\,{u}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}{r^{\prime}}\,{u}=t^{\prime}
, where
r\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}r^{\prime}
and
\neg\mathsf{abs}(r)
. Then,
\Phi
ends with rule

\mathtt{({app^{\mathcal{N}}_{p}})}
or
\mathtt{({app^{\mathcal{N}}_{c}})}
, and in either case the subterm
r
has an
associated typing derivation
\Phi_{r}
which verifies the hypothesis. Hence,
by the
i.h.
there exists a
\Phi_{r^{\prime}}
with proper counters. Thus, we can
derive
\Phi^{\prime}
applying the same rule as in
\Phi
to conclude.

•

t=\lambda{x}.{u}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}\lambda{x}.{u^{\prime}}=t^{\prime}
, where
r\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}r^{\prime}
. By the hypothesis we have necessarily
\sigma\in\mathtt{tt}

since
t
is an abstraction, then
t
is type with rule
\mathtt{({abs_{p}})}
. The
statement then immediately follows from the
i.h.

∎

Theorem 7

(Soundness)
.

If

{\Phi}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Gamma}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}},{\mathit{e}},{\mathit{s}})}{{t}:{\sigma}}}

is tight, then there exists
p
such that
p\in\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{n}}
and

t\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{n}}^{(\mathit{m},\mathit{e})}p
with
\mathit{m}

\mathtt{m}
-steps,
\mathit{e}

\mathtt{e}
-steps, and
|{p}|_{\mathtt{n}}=\mathit{s}
.

Proof.

We prove the statement for
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}
and then conclude for the general
notion of reduction
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}}
by the observation that all
reduction sequences to normal form have the same number of multiplicative and
exponential steps. Let

{\Phi}\rhd_{\mathcal{B}}{{\Gamma}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}},{\mathit{e}},{\mathit{s}})}{{t}:{\sigma}}}
.
We proceed by induction on
\mathit{m}+\mathit{e}
:

•

If
\mathit{m}+\mathit{e}=0
, then
\mathit{m}=\mathit{e}=0
and
Lem.
3
gives
t\in\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{n}}
. Moreover, by
Lem.
4
we get both
|{t}|_{\mathtt{n}}=\mathit{s}
. Thus, we
conclude with
p=t
.

•

If
\mathit{m}+\mathit{e}>0
, then
t\notin\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{n}}
holds by
Lem.
3
and thus there exists
t^{\prime}
such that
t\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}^{(1,0)}t^{\prime}
or
t\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}^{(0,1)}t^{\prime}
by
Prop.
1
. By
Lem.
6
there exists a type derivation

{\Phi^{\prime}}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Gamma}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}^{\prime}},{\mathit{e}^{\prime}},{\mathit{s}})}{{t^{\prime}}:{\sigma}}}

such that
1+\mathit{m}^{\prime}+\mathit{e}^{\prime}=\mathit{m}+\mathit{e}
. By the
i.h.
there exists
p\in\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{n}}
such that
t^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}^{(\mathit{m}^{\prime},\mathit{e}^{\prime})}p
with
\mathit{s}=|{p}|_{\mathtt{n}}
. Then
t\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}^{(1,0)}t^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}^{(\mathit{m}^{\prime},\mathit{e}^{\prime})}p

(resp.
t\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}^{(0,1)}t^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}^{(\mathit{m}^{\prime},\mathit{e}^{\prime})}p
) which
means
t\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}^{(\mathit{m},\mathit{e})}p
, as expected.

∎

Completeness.

Similarly, the completeness result relays on a series of intermediate lemmas:

Lemma 8
.

If
t\in\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{n}}
, then there is a tight derivation

{\Phi}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Gamma}\vdash^{({0},{0},{|{t}|_{\mathtt{n}}})}{{t}:{\sigma}}}
.

Proof.

By simultaneous induction on the following claims:

1.

If
t\in\mathsf{ne}_{\mathtt{n}}
, then there
exists a tight derivation

{\Phi}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Gamma}\vdash^{({0},{0},{|{t}|_{\mathtt{n}}})}{{t}:{\mathtt{n}}}}
.

2.

If
t\in\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{n}}
, then there
exists a tight derivation

{\Phi}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Gamma}\vdash^{({0},{0},{|{t}|_{\mathtt{n}}})}{{t}:{\mathtt{tt}}}}
.

•

t=x
. Then,
t\in\mathsf{ne}_{\mathtt{v}}\subseteq\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{v}}
and we conclude both
(
1
) and (
2
)
by
\mathtt{({var_{c}})}
since
|{x}|_{\mathtt{n}}=0
.

•

t={r}\,{u}
. Then,
t\in\mathsf{ne}_{\mathtt{n}}
or
t\in\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{n}}
implies

r\in\mathsf{ne}_{\mathtt{n}}
. By the
i.h.
(
1
) on
r
there
exists a tight derivation

{\Phi_{r}}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Gamma}\vdash^{({0},{0},{|{r}|_{\mathtt{n}}})}{{r}:{\mathtt{n}}}}
.
We conclude (
1
) by
\mathtt{({app^{\mathcal{N}}_{p}})}
,
obtaining
|{t}|_{\mathtt{n}}=|{r}|_{\mathtt{n}}+1
as expected. Then
(
2
) also holds.

•

t=\lambda{x}.{r}
. Then,
t\in\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{n}}
implies
r\in\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{n}}
. By
the
i.h.
(
2
) on
r
there exists a tight
derivation

{\Phi_{r}}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Gamma^{\prime}}\vdash^{({0},{0},{|{r}|_{\mathtt{n}}})}{{r}:{\mathtt{tt}}}}
.
We conclude (
2
) by using
\mathtt{({abs_{p}})}
,
thus obtaining
|{t}|_{\mathtt{n}}=|{r}|_{\mathtt{n}}+1
as expected.

•

t={r}[{x}\backslash{u}]\notin\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{n}}
. So this case does not apply.

∎

Lemma 9

(Anti-Substitution)
.

Let

{\Phi_{{{t}\left\{{x}\backslash{u}\right\}}}}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Gamma^{\prime}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}^{\prime}},{\mathit{e}^{\prime}},{\mathit{s}^{\prime}})}{{{{t}\left\{{x}\backslash{u}\right\}}}:{\tau}}}
.
Then, there exist

{\Phi_{t}}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Gamma;{x}:{[\sigma_{i}]_{i\in I}}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}},{\mathit{e}},{\mathit{s}})}{{t}:{\tau}}}

and

({\Phi^{i}_{u}}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Delta_{i}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}_{i}},{\mathit{e}_{i}},{\mathit{s}_{i}})}{{u}:{\sigma_{i}}}})_{i\in I}

such that
\Gamma^{\prime}={\Gamma}\mathrel{+_{i\in I}}{\Delta_{i}}
,
\mathit{m}^{\prime}=\mathit{m}+_{i\in I}{\mathit{m}_{i}}
,
\mathit{e}^{\prime}=\mathit{e}+_{i\in I}{\mathit{e}_{i}}
and
\mathit{s}^{\prime}=\mathit{s}+_{i\in I}{\mathit{s}_{i}}
.

Proof.

By induction on
t
.

•

t=x
. Then,
{{t}\left\{{x}\backslash{u}\right\}}=u
and we take

{\Phi_{x}}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{{x}:{[\sigma_{1}]}}\vdash^{({0},{0},{0})}{{x}:{\sigma_{1}}}}

and

{\Phi^{1}_{u}}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Gamma^{\prime}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}^{\prime}},{\mathit{e}^{\prime}},{\mathit{s}^{\prime}})}{{u}:{\sigma_{1}}}}

and the property holds.

•

t=y\neq x
. Then,
{{t}\left\{{x}\backslash{v}\right\}}=y
and we take

{\Phi_{y}}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{{y}:{[\tau]}}\vdash^{({0},{0},{0})}{{y}:{\tau}}}

so that
|I|=0
and the property holds.

•

In all the other cases the property holds by the
i.h.

∎

Lemma 10

(Exact Subject Expansion)
.

Let

{\Phi^{\prime}}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Gamma}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}^{\prime}},{\mathit{e}^{\prime}},{\mathit{s}})}{{t^{\prime}}:{\sigma}}}

be a tight derivation. If
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}t^{\prime}
, then there is

{\Phi}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Gamma}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}},{\mathit{e}},{\mathit{s}})}{{t}:{\sigma}}}

such that

1.

\mathit{m}^{\prime}=\mathit{m}-1
and
\mathit{e}^{\prime}=\mathit{e}
if
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}t^{\prime}
is an

\mathtt{m}
-step.

2.

\mathit{e}^{\prime}=\mathit{e}-1
and
\mathit{m}^{\prime}=\mathit{m}
if
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}t^{\prime}
is an

\mathtt{e}
-step.

Proof.

We actually prove the following stronger statement that allows us to reason
inductively:

Let
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}t^{\prime}
and

{\Phi^{\prime}}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Gamma}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}^{\prime}},{\mathit{e}^{\prime}},{\mathit{s}})}{{t^{\prime}}:{\sigma}}}

such that
\Gamma
is tight, and either
\sigma\in\mathtt{tt}
or

\neg\mathsf{abs}(t)
. Then, there exists

{\Phi}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Gamma}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}},{\mathit{e}},{\mathit{s}})}{{t}:{\sigma}}}

such that

1.

\mathit{m}^{\prime}=\mathit{m}-1
and
\mathit{e}^{\prime}=\mathit{e}
if
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}t^{\prime}
is an

\mathtt{m}
-step.

2.

\mathit{e}^{\prime}=\mathit{e}-1
and
\mathit{m}^{\prime}=\mathit{m}
if
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}t^{\prime}
is an

\mathtt{e}
-step.

We proceed by induction on
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}t^{\prime}
.

•

t={{\mathtt{L}}\langle{\lambda{x}.{r}}\rangle}\,{u}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}{\mathtt{L}}\langle{{r}[{x}\backslash{u}]}\rangle=t^{\prime}
is an
\mathtt{m}
-step.
We proceed by induction on
\mathtt{L}
. We only show here the case
\mathtt{L}=\Box
as the inductive case is straightforward.

Then,
t^{\prime}={r}[{x}\backslash{u}]
and
\Phi^{\prime}
has the following form:

\displaystyle\penalty{\Phi_{r}}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Gamma^{\prime};{x}:{[\sigma_{i}]_{i\in I}}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}_{1}},{\mathit{e}_{1}},{\mathit{s}_{1}})}{{r}:{\sigma}}}\quad({\Phi^{i}_{u}}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Delta_{i}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}^{i}_{2}},{\mathit{e}^{i}_{2}},{\mathit{s}^{i}_{2}})}{{u}:{\sigma_{i}}}})_{i\in I}

\mathtt{({es^{\mathcal{N}}_{c}})}

\displaystyle{{\Gamma^{\prime}}\mathrel{+_{i\in I}}{\Delta_{i}}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}_{1}+_{i\in I}{\mathit{m}^{i}_{2}}},{1+\mathit{e}_{1}+_{i\in I}{\mathit{e}^{i}_{2}}},{\mathit{s}_{1}+_{i\in I}{\mathit{s}^{i}_{2}}})}{{{r}[{x}\backslash{u}]}:{\sigma}}

with
\mathit{m}^{\prime}=\mathit{m}_{1}+_{i\in I}{\mathit{m}^{i}_{2}}
,
\mathit{e}^{\prime}=1+\mathit{e}_{1}+_{i\in I}{\mathit{e}^{i}_{2}}
and
\mathit{s}=\mathit{s}_{1}+_{i\in I}{\mathit{s}^{i}_{2}}
. We
conclude with the following type derivation

\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty{\Phi_{r}}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Gamma^{\prime};{x}:{[\sigma_{i}]_{i\in I}}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}_{1}},{\mathit{e}_{1}},{\mathit{s}_{1}})}{{r}:{\sigma}}}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=129.84175pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{({abs_{c}})}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 8.88776pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\Gamma^{\prime}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}_{1}},{\mathit{e}_{1}},{\mathit{s}_{1}})}{{\lambda{x}.{r}}:{{[\sigma_{i}]_{i\in I}}\to{\sigma}}}$}}}}\enskip\penalty\quad({\Phi^{i}_{u}}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Delta_{i}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}^{i}_{2}},{\mathit{e}^{i}_{2}},{\mathit{s}^{i}_{2}})}{{u}:{\sigma_{i}}}})_{i\in I}

\mathtt{({app^{\mathcal{N}}_{c}})}

\displaystyle{\Phi}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{{{\Gamma^{\prime}}\mathrel{\setminus\!\!\setminus}{x}}\mathrel{+}{\Delta}}\vdash^{({1+\mathit{m}_{1}+_{i\in I}{\mathit{m}^{i}_{2}}},{1+\mathit{e}_{1}+_{i\in I}{\mathit{e}^{i}_{2}}},{\mathit{s}_{1}+_{i\in I}{\mathit{s}^{i}_{2}}})}{{{(\lambda{x}.{r})}\,{u}}:{\sigma}}}

taking
\mathit{m}=1+\mathit{m}_{1}+_{i\in I}{\mathit{m}^{i}_{2}}=\mathit{m}^{\prime}+1
and

\mathit{e}=1+\mathit{e}_{1}+_{i\in I}{\mathit{e}^{i}_{2}}=\mathit{e}^{\prime}
.

•

t={r}[{x}\backslash{u}]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}{{r}\left\{{x}\backslash{u}\right\}}=t^{\prime}
is an

\mathtt{e}
-step. By Lem.
9
there exist type
derivations

{\Phi_{r}}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Gamma^{\prime};{x}:{[\sigma_{i}]_{i\in I}}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}_{1}},{\mathit{e}_{1}},{\mathit{s}_{1}})}{{r}:{\sigma}}}

and

({\Phi^{i}_{u}}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Delta_{i}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}^{i}_{2}},{\mathit{e}^{i}_{2}},{\mathit{s}^{i}_{2}})}{{u}:{\sigma_{i}}}})_{i\in I}

such that
\Gamma={\Gamma^{\prime}}\mathrel{+_{i\in I}}{\Delta_{i}}
,
\mathit{m}^{\prime}=\mathit{m}_{1}+_{i\in I}{\mathit{m}^{i}_{2}}
,
\mathit{e}^{\prime}=\mathit{e}_{1}+_{i\in I}{\mathit{e}^{i}_{2}}
and
\mathit{s}=\mathit{s}_{1}+_{i\in I}{\mathit{s}^{i}_{2}}
. We conclude by
\mathtt{({es^{\mathcal{N}}_{c}})}
with the
following type derivation

\displaystyle\penalty{\Phi_{r}}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Gamma^{\prime};{x}:{[\sigma_{i}]_{i\in I}}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}_{1}},{\mathit{e}_{1}},{\mathit{s}_{1}})}{{r}:{\sigma}}}\quad({\Phi^{i}_{u}}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Delta_{i}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}^{i}_{2}},{\mathit{e}^{i}_{2}},{\mathit{s}^{i}_{2}})}{{u}:{\sigma_{i}}}})_{i\in I}

\mathtt{({es^{\mathcal{N}}_{c}})}

\displaystyle{\Phi}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{{\Gamma^{\prime}}\mathrel{+_{i\in I}}{\Delta_{i}}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}_{1}+_{i\in I}{\mathit{m}^{i}_{2}}},{1+\mathit{e}_{1}+_{i\in I}{\mathit{e}^{i}_{2}}},{\mathit{s}_{1}+_{i\in I}{\mathit{s}^{i}_{2}}})}{{{r}[{x}\backslash{u}]}:{\sigma}}}

taking
\mathit{m}=\mathit{m}_{1}+_{i\in I}{\mathit{m}^{i}_{2}}=\mathit{m}^{\prime}
and
\mathit{e}=1+\mathit{e}_{1}+_{i\in I}{\mathit{e}^{i}_{2}}=\mathit{e}^{\prime}+1
.

•

t={r}\,{u}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}{r^{\prime}}\,{u}=t^{\prime}
, where
r\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}r^{\prime}
and
\neg\mathsf{abs}(r)
. Then,
\Phi^{\prime}
ends with rule

\mathtt{({app^{\mathcal{N}}_{p}})}
or
\mathtt{({app^{\mathcal{N}}_{c}})}
, and in either case the subterm
r^{\prime}
has
an associated typing derivation
\Phi_{r^{\prime}}
which verifies the hypothesis
since
\neg\mathsf{abs}(r)
. Hence, by
i.h.
there exists a
\Phi_{r}
with proper
counters such that we can derive
\Phi
applying the same rule as in
\Phi^{\prime}

to conclude.

•

t=\lambda{x}.{u}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}\lambda{x}.{u^{\prime}}=t^{\prime}
, where
r\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}r^{\prime}
. By the hypothesis we have necessarily
\sigma\in\mathtt{tt}

since
t^{\prime}
is an abstraction, then
t^{\prime}
is type with rule
\mathtt{({abs_{p}})}
.
The statement then immediately follows from the
i.h.

∎

Theorem 11

(Completeness)
.

If
t\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{n}}^{(\mathit{m},\mathit{e})}p
with
p\in\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{n}}
, then
there exists a tight type derivation

{\Phi}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Gamma}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}},{\mathit{e}},{|{p}|_{\mathtt{v}}})}{{t}:{\sigma}}}
.

Proof.

As for soundness (Thm.
7
) we prove the
statement for
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}
and then conclude for the general notion of
reduction
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}}
. Let
t\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}^{(\mathit{m},\mathit{e})}p
. We
proceed by induction on
\mathit{m}+\mathit{e}
:

•

If
\mathit{m}+\mathit{e}=0
, then
\mathit{m}=\mathit{e}=0
and thus
t=p
,
which implies
t\in\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{n}}
. Lem.
8
allows to
conclude.

•

If
\mathit{m}+\mathit{e}>0
, then there is
t^{\prime}
such that
t\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}^{(1,0)}t^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}^{(\mathit{m}-1,\mathit{e})}p
or
t\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}^{(0,1)}t^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}^{(\mathit{m},\mathit{e}-1)}p
. By the
i.h.
there is
a tight derivation

{\Phi^{\prime}}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Gamma}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}^{\prime}},{\mathit{e}^{\prime}},{|{p}|_{\mathtt{v}}})}{{t^{\prime}}:{\sigma}}}

such
\mathit{m}^{\prime}+\mathit{e}^{\prime}=\mathit{m}+\mathit{e}-1
.
Lem.
10
gives a tight derivation

{\Phi}\rhd_{\mathcal{N}}{{\Gamma}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}^{\prime\prime}},{\mathit{e}^{\prime\prime}},{|{p}|_{\mathtt{v}}})}{{t}:{\sigma}}}

such
\mathit{m}^{\prime\prime}+\mathit{e}^{\prime\prime}=\mathit{m}^{\prime}+\mathit{e}^{\prime}+1
. We then have
\mathit{m}^{\prime\prime}+\mathit{e}^{\prime\prime}=\mathit{m}+\mathit{e}
. The fact that
\mathit{m}^{\prime\prime}=\mathit{m}
and
\mathit{e}^{\prime\prime}=\mathit{e}
holds by a simple case analysis.

∎

Notice that the previous theorem is stated by using the general notion of
reduction
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}}
, but the proofs are done using the
deterministic reduction
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dn}}
. The equivalence holds because any two
different reduction paths to normal form have the same number of multiplicative
and exponential steps, as already remarked.

Example 12
.

Consider
t_{0}={{{\tt K}}\,{({z}\,{{\tt I}})}}\,{({{\tt I}}\,{{\tt I}})}
that

\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}}
-reduces in 2
\mathtt{m}
-steps and 2
\mathtt{e}
-steps to the term

{z}\,{{\tt I}}\in\mathsf{no}_{\mathtt{n}}
, whose
\mathtt{n}
-size is 1:

t_{0}={\underline{{{\tt K}}\,{({z}\,{{\tt I}})}}}\,{({{\tt I}}\,{{\tt I}})}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dB}}\underline{{{(\lambda{y}.{x})}[{x}\backslash{{z}\,{{\tt I}}}]}\,{({{\tt I}}\,{{\tt I}})}}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dB}}\underline{{{x}[{y}\backslash{{{\tt I}}\,{{\tt I}}}]}[{x}\backslash{{z}\,{{\tt I}}}]}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sn}}\underline{{({z}\,{{\tt I}})}[{y}\backslash{{{\tt I}}\,{{\tt I}}}]}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sn}}{z}\,{{\tt I}}

System
\mathcal{N}
admits a tight type derivation for
t_{0}
with the
expected final counter
(2,2,1)
:

\mathtt{({var_{c}})}

\displaystyle{{x}:{[\mathtt{n}]}}\vdash^{({0},{0},{0})}{{x}:{\mathtt{n}}}

\mathtt{({abs_{c}})}

\displaystyle{{x}:{[\mathtt{n}]}}\vdash^{({0},{0},{0})}{{\lambda{y}.{x}}:{{[\,]}\to{\mathtt{n}}}}

\mathtt{({abs_{c}})}

\displaystyle{}\vdash^{({0},{0},{0})}{{{\tt K}}:{{[\mathtt{n}]}\to{{[\,]}\to{\mathtt{n}}}}}

\mathtt{({var_{c}})}

\displaystyle{{z}:{[\mathtt{n}]}}\vdash^{({0},{0},{0})}{{z}:{\mathtt{n}}}

\mathtt{({app^{\mathcal{N}}_{p}})}

\displaystyle{{z}:{[\mathtt{n}]}}\vdash^{({0},{0},{1})}{{{z}\,{{\tt I}}}:{\mathtt{n}}}

\mathtt{({app^{\mathcal{N}}_{c}})}

\displaystyle{{z}:{[\mathtt{n}]}}\vdash^{({1},{1},{1})}{{{{\tt K}}\,{({z}\,{{\tt I}})}}:{{[\,]}\to{\mathtt{n}}}}

\mathtt{({app^{\mathcal{N}}_{c}})}

\displaystyle{{z}:{[\mathtt{n}]}}\vdash^{({2},{2},{1})}{{{{{\tt K}}\,{({z}\,{{\tt I}})}}\,{({{\tt I}}\,{{\tt I}})}}:{\mathtt{n}}}

4
Tight Call-by-Value

In this section we introduce a tight system
\mathcal{V}
for CBV which
captures exact measures for
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{v}}
-reduction sequences. Here
we do not only distinguish length of reduction sequences from size of normal
forms, but also discriminate multiplicative from exponential steps. Moreover,
we establish a precise relation between the counters in CBV and their
counterparts in the
\lambda!
-calculus (
cf.
Sec.
8
).
This constitutes one of the main contributions of the present work.

The grammar of types of system
\mathcal{V}
is given by:

(Tight Types)

\mathtt{tt}

::=

\mathtt{n}\mid\mathtt{vl}\mid\mathtt{vr}

(Types)

\sigma,\tau

::=

\mathtt{tt}\mid\mathcal{M}\mid{\mathcal{M}}\to{\sigma}

(Multitypes)

\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}

::=

[\sigma_{i}]_{i\in I}
where
I
is a finite set

Indeed, apart from the constant
\mathtt{n}
also used in CBN, we now
introduce constants
\mathtt{vl}
and
\mathtt{vr}
, typing respectively, terms
reducing to values; and terms reducing to persistent variables that are not
acting as values (
i.e.
they are applied to some argument to produce neutral
terms). Notice that we remove the base type
\mathtt{a}
from CBN, since
abstractions are in particular values, so they will be typed with

\mathtt{vl}
. The notions of
tightness
for multitypes, contexts and
derivations are exactly the same we used for CBN.

A type system for CBV must type variables without knowing yet the future role
they are going to play (head of neutral term or value). This is one of the
main difficulties behind the definition of such a system. Besides that, the
system must also be able to count multiplicative and exponential steps
independently. The resulting type system
\mathcal{V}
for CBV is given by
the typing rules in Fig.
3

and
4
, distinguishing between
persistent and consuming rules resp. As a matter of notation, given a
tight type
\mathtt{tt}_{0}
we write
\overline{\mathtt{tt}_{0}}
to denote
a tight type different from
\mathtt{tt}_{0}
. Thus for instance,

\overline{\mathtt{vr}}\in\{\mathtt{vl},\mathtt{n}\}
.

\kern-10.00002pt\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 36.44441pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=72.88882pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{({var_{p}})}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{{x}:{[\mathtt{vr}]}}\vdash^{({0},{0},{0})}{{x}:{\mathtt{vr}}}$}}}}\quad{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 24.17015pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=48.3403pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{({val_{p}})}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{}\vdash^{({0},{0},{0})}{{x}:{\mathtt{vl}}}$}}}}\quad{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 30.28128pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=60.56256pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{({abs^{\mathcal{V}}_{p}})}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{}\vdash^{({0},{0},{0})}{{\lambda{x}.{t}}:{\mathtt{vl}}}$}}}}\\
\\
{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 6.53471pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty{\Gamma}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}},{\mathit{e}},{\mathit{s}})}{{t}:{\overline{\mathtt{vl}}}}\kern 5.0pt{\Delta}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}^{\prime}},{\mathit{e}^{\prime}},{\mathit{s}^{\prime}})}{{u}:{\overline{\mathtt{vr}}}}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=123.2369pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{({app^{\mathcal{V}}_{p}})}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{{\Gamma}\mathrel{+}{\Delta}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}+\mathit{m}^{\prime}},{\mathit{e}+\mathit{e}^{\prime}},{\mathit{s}+\mathit{s}^{\prime}+1})}{{{t}\,{u}}:{\mathtt{n}}}$}}}}\\
\\
{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty{\Gamma}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}},{\mathit{e}},{\mathit{s}})}{{t}:{\tau}}\kern 5.0pt{\Delta}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}^{\prime}},{\mathit{e}^{\prime}},{\mathit{s}^{\prime}})}{{u}:{\mathtt{n}}}\kern 5.0pt\mathsf{tight}(\Gamma(x))$}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=157.08762pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{({es_{p}})}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 1.15619pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{{({\Gamma}\mathrel{\setminus\!\!\setminus}{x})}\mathrel{+}{\Delta}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}+\mathit{m}^{\prime}},{\mathit{e}+\mathit{e}^{\prime}},{\mathit{s}+\mathit{s}^{\prime}})}{{{t}[{x}\backslash{u}]}:{\tau}}$}}}}\end{array}

Figure 3:
System
\mathcal{V}
for the Call-by-Value Calculus: Persistent Typing Rules.

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 29.2084pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vphantom{\Gamma}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=64.66682pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{({var^{\mathcal{V}}_{c}})}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{{x}:{\mathcal{M}}}\vdash^{({0},{1},{0})}{{x}:{\mathcal{M}}}$}}}}\qquad{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty{\Gamma}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}},{\mathit{e}},{\mathit{s}})}{{t}:{[{\mathcal{M}}\to{\tau}]}}\quad{\Delta}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}^{\prime}},{\mathit{e}^{\prime}},{\mathit{s}^{\prime}})}{{u}:{\mathcal{M}}}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=143.42795pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{({app^{\mathcal{V}}_{c}})}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 7.19446pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{{\Gamma}\mathrel{+}{\Delta}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}+\mathit{m}^{\prime}+1},{\mathit{e}+\mathit{e}^{\prime}-1},{\mathit{s}+\mathit{s}^{\prime}})}{{{t}\,{u}}:{\tau}}$}}}}\\
\\
{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty{\Gamma}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}},{\mathit{e}},{\mathit{s}})}{{t}:{[{\mathcal{M}}\to{\tau}]}}\quad{\Delta}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}^{\prime}},{\mathit{e}^{\prime}},{\mathit{s}^{\prime}})}{{u}:{\mathtt{n}}}\quad\mathsf{tight}(\mathcal{M})$}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=186.23352pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{({appt^{\mathcal{V}}_{c}})}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 28.59724pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{{\Gamma}\mathrel{+}{\Delta}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}+\mathit{m}^{\prime}+1},{\mathit{e}+\mathit{e}^{\prime}-1},{\mathit{s}+\mathit{s}^{\prime}})}{{{t}\,{u}}:{\tau}}$}}}}\\
\\
{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 68.773pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty({{\Gamma_{i}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}_{i}},{\mathit{e}_{i}},{\mathit{s}_{i}})}{{t}:{\tau_{i}}}})_{i\in I}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=222.59183pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{({abs^{\mathcal{V}}_{c}})}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{{}\mathrel{+_{i\in I}}{{\Gamma_{i}}\mathrel{\setminus\!\!\setminus}{x}}}\vdash^{({+_{i\in I}{\mathit{m}_{i}}},{1+_{i\in I}{\mathit{e}_{i}}},{+_{i\in I}{\mathit{s}_{i}}})}{{\lambda{x}.{t}}:{[{\Gamma_{i}(x)}\to{\tau_{i}}]_{i\in I}}}$}}}}\\
\\
{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 10.24658pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty{\Gamma}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}},{\mathit{e}},{\mathit{s}})}{{t}:{\sigma}}\quad{\Delta}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}^{\prime}},{\mathit{e}^{\prime}},{\mathit{s}^{\prime}})}{{u}:{\Gamma(x)}}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=156.11783pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{({es_{c}})}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{{({\Gamma}\mathrel{\setminus\!\!\setminus}{x})}\mathrel{+}{\Delta}}\vdash^{({\mathit{m}+\mathit{m}^{\prime}},{\mathit{e}+\mathit{e}^{\prime}},{\mathit{s}+\mathit{s}^{\prime}})}{{{t}[{x}\backslash{u}]}:{\sigma}}$}}}}\end{array}

Figure 4:
System
\mathcal{V}
for the Call-by-Value Calculus: Consuming Typing Rules.

Some rules deserve a comment. A difficult property to be statically captured by
the counters is that an exponential step can only be generated by the meeting
of a substitution constructor with an appropriate value argument. This remark
leads to the introduction of different rules for typing variables, depending on
the role they play (to be a value or not). Indeed, there are three axioms for
variables:
\mathtt{({var_{p}})}
typing variables that will persist as the head of
a neutral term;
\mathtt{({val_{p}})}
typing variable values that may be substituted
by other values,
i.e.
they are
placeholders
for future persistent values;
and
\mathtt{({var^{\mathcal{V}}_{c}})}
typing variable values that, in particular, may be consumed
as arguments. Consuming values are always typed with multitypes. Rule

\mathtt{({app^{\mathcal{V}}_{p}})}
types neutral applications,
i.e.
the left premise has type

\mathtt{vr}
or
\mathtt{n}
.
Rule
\mathtt{({abs^{\mathcal{V}}_{c}})}
increases the second counter, just like
\mathtt{({var^{\mathcal{V}}_{c}})}
,
typing a value that is consumed as an argument. Rules
\mathtt{({app^{\mathcal{V}}_{c}})}
and

\mathtt{({appt^{\mathcal{V}}_{c}})}
increment the first counter because the (consuming) application
